=== PROMPT VARIANT ===
r1

=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
An integer sequence where no two adjacent elements are the same is called a good sequence.
You are given two good sequences of length N: A=(A_1,A_2,\dots,A_N) and B=(B_1,B_2,\dots,B_N). Each element of A and B is between 0 and M-1, inclusive.
You can perform the following operations on A any number of times, possibly zero:

- Choose an integer i between 1  and N, inclusive, and perform one of the following:
- Set A_i \leftarrow (A_i + 1) \bmod M.
- Set A_i \leftarrow (A_i - 1) \bmod M. Here, (-1) \bmod M = M - 1.



However, you cannot perform an operation that makes A no longer a good sequence.
Determine if it is possible to make A equal to B, and if it is possible, find the minimum number of operations required to do so.

Input

The input is given from Standard Input in the following format:
N M
A_1 A_2 \dots A_N
B_1 B_2 \dots B_N

Output

If the goal is unachievable, print -1.
Otherwise, print the minimum number of operations required as an integer.

Constraints


- 2 \leq N \leq 2 \times 10^5
- 2 \leq M \leq 10^6
- 0\leq A_i,B_i< M(1\leq i\leq N)
- A_i\ne A_{i+1}(1\leq i\leq N-1)
- B_i\ne B_{i+1}(1\leq i\leq N-1)
- All input values are integers.

Sample Input 1

3 9
2 0 1
4 8 1

Sample Output 1

3

You can achieve the goal in three operations as follows:

- Set A_1 \leftarrow (A_1 + 1) \bmod M. Now A = (3, 0, 1).
- Set A_2 \leftarrow (A_2 - 1) \bmod M. Now A = (3, 8, 1).
- Set A_1 \leftarrow (A_1 + 1) \bmod M. Now A = (4, 8, 1).

It is impossible to achieve the goal in two or fewer operations, so the answer is 3.
For example, you cannot set A_2 \leftarrow (A_2 + 1) \bmod M in the first operation, because it would make A = (2, 1, 1), which is not a good sequence.

Sample Input 2

3 9
1 8 2
1 8 2

Sample Output 2

0

A and B might be equal from the beginning.

Sample Input 3

24 182
128 115 133 52 166 92 164 119 143 99 54 162 86 2 59 166 24 78 81 5 109 67 172 99
136 103 136 28 16 52 2 85 134 64 123 74 64 28 85 161 19 74 14 110 125 104 180 75

Sample Output 3

811

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Try to avoid patterns like these:
- spending too long comparing nearby plans before committing to one workable approach;
- returning from code to broad algorithm discussion without a concrete contradiction;
- turning one local doubt into a full restart when the current draft only needs a small patch.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

Let's attempt to prove constructively for ourselves.

We need show: For any two valid integer tuples X, Y (same length, gaps in [1,M-1]), there exists a sequence of unit coordinate moves from X to Y of length L1 = Σ |X_i-Y_i|.

This is equivalent to: We can move each coordinate monotonically from X_i to Y_i (no overshoot) in some order, maintaining gap constraints. Since if any coordinate moves opposite direction, extra moves would exceed L1. So we need a monotone schedule.

Let D_i = Y_i - X_i (can be positive, negative, zero). We need order unit moves such that at each step, for each coordinate i, its displacement d_i(t) is between 0 and D_i if D_i>0, or between D_i and 0 if D_i<0, and moves by ±1. The gap between i and i+1 at time t:
g_i(t) = X_{i+1}+d_{i+1}(t) - (X_i+d_i(t)) = g_i^0 + (d_{i+1}-d_i).
Need 1 ≤ g_i^0 + (d_{i+1}-d_i) ≤ M-1.
At final, g_i^1 = Y_{i+1}-Y_i also in range.

This is a discrete scheduling problem. We can choose d_i(t) as monotone paths. Is there always a path in the box of d_i? The set of possible displacement vectors d with 0≤d_i≤D_i (or D_i≤d_i≤0) and constraints on differences is an integral polytope. We need a lattice path from 0 to D using unit steps in coordinate directions staying in this polytope. This is exactly the existence of a monotone path in an integral polytope. For any two vertices? Let's think about the proof of L1 distance more rigorously, in case final answer maybe includes explanation? User asked final code only, but we need be confident.

Potential issue: The graph of valid lifts with gaps in [1,M-1] might have distance L1 only if the two endpoints are in the same "orthant" (i.e., no coordinate needs to move both directions). But L1 distance is sum absolute differences; any path of length L1 implies each coordinate moves monotonically. Is it always possible to move monotonically? We found examples yes. Need prove.

Seems monotone path exists by a greedy algorithm: At each step, move any coordinate i that is "ready": if D_i>0, moving right is safe if X_i+1 < X_{i+1} and X_{i+1}? Actually moving right increases gap to left, decreases gap to right. Need ensure left gap ≤M-1 after move and right gap ≥1. If not ready, move right neighbor right first or left neighbor? This is like sorting.

The state space is a distributive lattice: define for each coordinate i and each possible "level" maybe. The distance in a distributive lattice's Hasse diagram equals rank difference? Wait if the graph is the cover graph of a distributive lattice, the shortest path distance between two elements is not always rank difference? In a distributive lattice, the Hasse diagram is connected and the distance (number of cover relations) between two elements can be greater than rank difference if they are incomparable? Actually in any graded poset, the distance in the Hasse diagram between two elements is at least |rank(a)-rank(b)|, but can be larger. For example Boolean lattice, distance between ∅ and {1,2} is 2 = rank diff; between {1} and {2} is 2, rank diff0. So not L1. Our L1 distance is like rank difference in a product of chains? If we can map each state to a multiset of chains, distance may be L1.

Maybe the valid tuples are in bijection with order ideals of a poset that is a disjoint union of chains? Let's find such a bijection. Constraints X_{i+1}-X_i ∈ [1,M-1]. Let define for each i and integer k, a binary variable? Since X_i are integer, we can represent X_i as number of "tokens" up to level. The constraints are that for each adjacent pair, their "threshold" counts differ by at most M-1. This is exactly the set of integer points of a "polymatroid" maybe. The graph with unit coordinate moves is the set of integer points of a generalized permutahedron; such polytopes are known to be "lattice path polytopes" and the graph distance between vertices? But all integer points are vertices? Actually the graph includes all integer points, not just vertices.

Let's attempt a direct constructive proof using "moving from left to right" or "right to left" based on target displacements.

Given D_i = Y_i - X_i. We can define a potential Φ = Σ |current_i - Y_i|. Each move toward target decreases by1. Need avoid invalid. We need show there is always at least one coordinate i with D_i>0 that can move +1 safely, or D_i<0 that can move -1 safely, until done.

Consider a coordinate i with D_i>0 (needs move right). Moving it right is invalid if:
- X_i+1 = X_{i+1} (right gap 0) OR X_i+1 - X_{i-1} = M (left gap M). The second means X_i - X_{i-1} = M-1 and moving right increases left gap to M. Equivalently left neighbor is exactly M-1 behind.
If blocked by right gap (X_i+1 = X_{i+1}), then D_{i+1} must be >0? Since Y_i < Y_{i+1} (valid), if X_i+1 = X_{i+1} and D_i>0, then X_i < Y_i. Need show D_{i+1}>0. Suppose D_{i+1}≤0 (X_{i+1} ≥ Y_{i+1}). Then X_i+1 = X_{i+1} ≥ Y_{i+1} > Y_i (since Y_{i+1}≥Y_i+1). So X_i ≥ Y_i, contradiction D_i>0. Thus right neighbor also needs move right. So we can postpone to right.
If blocked by left gap M (X_i - X_{i-1}=M-1), then moving right would make left gap M. Since X_i < Y_i? Need maybe D_{i-1}<0? Let's see if left gap is M-1, then X_{i-1}=X_i-(M-1). If D_i>0, X_i < Y_i. Since Y_i - Y_{i-1} ≤ M-1, we have Y_{i-1} ≥ Y_i-(M-1) > X_i-(M-1)=X_{i-1}. Thus D_{i-1}>0? Wait Y_{i-1} > X_{i-1}, so D_{i-1}>0. So left neighbor also needs move right. Then moving i right blocked by left gap means left neighbor should move right first. This suggests if a right-moving coordinate is blocked, there is another right-moving coordinate to the left or right. Could lead to cycle? But since finite, there should be an unblocked right-moving coordinate at an extreme.

Similarly for left-moving.

We can choose a right-moving coordinate with minimal X_i? Or maximal? Let's think.

If there is any D_i>0. Let i be the rightmost coordinate with D_i>0? For i=N, moving right only affects left gap; blocked if X_N - X_{N-1}=M-1. Then as above D_{N-1}>0, contradiction rightmost. Now, compute min_K L1 efficiently. We can sort x_i. But maybe we can compute K by median of x_i // M? Need implement robustly.

Let's define function calc(K): sum(abs(p[i] - (q[i] + K*M)) for i in range(N)). Since p,q lists length N. We can compute for candidate K.

Candidate K selection:
- Compute xs = [p[i]-q[i] for i in range(N)]
- Sort xs.
- Let med = xs[N//2] (upper median). K0 = med // M (floor division).
- Candidates = {K0-2, K0-1, K0, K0+1, K0+2} maybe also K0+? If med negative, floor okay. But if optimal K is far from med//M? We argued no. Let's test with even N and median interval [100.1, 200.1], upper median=200.1, med//M=200, optimal integer in interval includes 101..200, so 200 works. So K0=upper_median//M is always an optimal integer? Let's prove: Let z_high = x_high/M. K0=floor(z_high). If z_high integer, K0=z_high in median interval (since z_low≤z_high), optimal. If z_high not integer, K0 < z_high < K0+1. If z_low ≤ K0, then K0 ∈ [z_low,z_high], optimal. If z_low > K0, then the entire median interval lies in (K0, K0+1). The real minimizers are [z_low,z_high]. For integer K, the minimizer is either K0 or K0+1? Since K0 < z_low ≤ z_high < K0+1. The function g(t) is minimized on the interval; for t outside, it increases linearly as distance from interval. Now, final code structure:
import sys
def main():
    input = sys.stdin.readline
    N,M = map(int,input().split())
    A = list(map(int,input().split()))
    B = list(map(int,input().split()))
    if M == 2:
        print(0 if A == B else -1)
        return
    p = [0]*N
    p[0] = A[0]
    for i in range(N-1):
        p[i+1] = p[i] + (A[i+1] - A[i]) % M
    q = [0]*N
    q[0] = B[0]
    for i in range(N-1):
        q[i+1] = q[i] + (B[i+1] - B[i]) % M
    xs = [p[i] - q[i] for i in range(N)]
    xs.sort()
    med = xs[N//2]  # upper median
    k0 = med // M
    ans = None
    for K in range(k0-2, k0+3):
        # compute sum abs(p_i - (q_i + K*M))
        # Could loop over p,q
        s = 0
        KM = K*M
        # local variables for speed
        for pi, qi in zip(p,q):
            diff = pi - qi - KM
            if diff < 0: diff = -diff
            s += diff
        if ans is None or s < ans:
            ans = s
    print(ans)

If K0 >= l, then K0 is in interval (or if K0<l? no). If K0 >= l, K0 optimal. If K0 < l, then interval lies in (K0, K0+1) (since l≤u<K0+1). The integer minimizer is either K0 or K0+1. So K0 and K0+1 suffice. If u integer, K0=u in interval, optimal. Thus K0 and K0+1 enough. For odd n, median unique z_med. K0=floor(z_med). If z_med integer, K0 optimal. Else interval point, integer minimizer K0 or K0+1. So K0 and K0+1 enough. Therefore ±2 definitely enough. Good.

Now, let's revisit the L1 distance proof in the context of M>=3. Is there any case where the target lift Q+KM is valid but the shortest path in the infinite graph is less than L1 due to moving a coordinate "the wrong way" and then another? L1 is a lower bound because each unit move changes one coordinate by ±1, so total moves ≥ Σ |initial_i - final_i|. If a coordinate moves back and forth, total > L1. Thus any path has length ≥ L1. We need show there exists path length exactly L1. We have strong empirical evidence. Need maybe prove via "the graph is isometric" for the polytope. Let's try to find a formal argument to be confident.

The set S = {X ∈ Z^N : 1 ≤ X_{i+1}-X_i ≤ M-1}. This is the set of integer points of a convex polyhedron. The graph G has edges between points differing by ±e_i. We want G is isometrically embedded into L1 (i.e., distance = L1). This is true if S is a "Cartesian product of intervals" under some linear transformation? Let's find a bijection to a product of chains.

Consider representing X by its "level sets". For each integer t, define S_t = {i : X_i ≥ t}. Since X_i increasing, S_t is a suffix? Actually X_1 ≤ X_2 ≤ ...? Wait X_i is increasing, so if X_i ≥ t then all later j>i have X_j ≥ X_i+1 ≥ t+1? At least >t, so yes X_j ≥ t. Thus S_t is a suffix {i, i+1, ..., N} for some i (possibly empty). The condition X_{i+1}-X_i ≤ M-1 means that the suffix cannot jump? Specifically, for each t, the set of indices with X_i < t is a prefix. The length of suffix maybe changes by at most? Let's define for each level t, a cut position c_t = number of i with X_i < t (0..N). Since X_i increasing, c_t is nondecreasing in t. X_{i+1}-X_i ≤ M-1 implies that between levels? If X_i < t ≤ X_{i+1}, then gap? Not sure.

Alternatively, define Y_i = X_i - i. Then Y_i nondecreasing. The upper bound X_{i+1}-X_i ≤ M-1 becomes Y_{i+1} - Y_i ≤ M-2. So Y is a nondecreasing sequence with increments ≤ M-2. This is the set of integer points of a "lecture hall" polytope? Not product.

Maybe use "height" h_i = X_i + (N-i)(M-1)? No.

Let's attempt to prove existence by induction on N.

Claim: For any valid X,Y, there is a monotone path. We can try to move the last coordinate X_N to Y_N first if Y_N ≥ X_N, or maybe not. If Y_N > X_N, moving X_N right is safe unless gap g_{N-1}=M-1 (would exceed). If gap g_{N-1}=M-1 and Y_N > X_N, then since Y_N - Y_{N-1} ≤ M-1 and Y_N > X_N, we have Y_{N-1} ≥ Y_N-(M-1) > X_N-(M-1)=X_{N-1}, so Y_{N-1} > X_{N-1}. Thus N-1 also needs move right. We can recursively move N-1? This suggests an algorithm: To increase a suffix, process from left to right? For all right-moving, we can move the leftmost right-moving coordinate? In example X=[0,1,4], Y=[2,3,5], leftmost right-moving i=1? D1=2, moving p1 right blocked by right gap? X1+1=1 equals X2=1, so blocked. So not leftmost. Maybe process from right to left but if blocked, move left neighbor. This is like carrying in addition. It will terminate.

For mixed signs, we can handle positive and negative displacements separately? Since moving a coordinate right may be blocked by a left neighbor that also needs move right; moving left may be blocked by right neighbor that also needs move left. Positive and negative moves don't conflict? If coordinate i needs move right and i+1 needs move left, they may move toward each other and risk collision. But final Y_i < Y_{i+1}, so there is room. Need schedule.

Maybe use a "greedy" algorithm based on the difference D_i:
- If D_i > 0, eventually move right; if D_i < 0, move left.
- We can perform moves in any order as long as safe. Is there always a safe move? Suppose no coordinate can move toward target. Then for every i with D_i>0, moving right is blocked; for every i with D_i<0, moving left is blocked. Can this happen? Let's analyze.

For D_i>0, blocked right means either X_i+1 = X_{i+1} (right gap 1) or X_i - X_{i-1} = M-1 (left gap M-1). If right gap1, then D_{i+1}>0 as shown. If left gap M-1, then D_{i-1}>0 as shown. Thus each blocked right-moving coordinate points to an adjacent right-moving coordinate (right if right gap1, left if left gap M-1). This forms a directed graph on right-moving indices. Since finite, there is a cycle or a sink? If every right-moving coordinate is blocked, following pointers gives a cycle. Can a cycle occur? Suppose i points right to i+1 (right gap1), and i+1 points left to i (left gap M-1) maybe? If X_{i+1}-X_i=1 and X_{i+1}-X_i=M-1 simultaneously impossible for M>2. But a longer cycle? Let's see pointers alternate directions. Could have i -> i+1 (gap1), i+1 -> i+2 (gap1), ..., j -> j-1 (gap M-1)? Need possible. Example M=5, gaps: g_i=1, g_{i+1}=1, ..., g_{j-1}=4. Then i points right, j points left. Could form cycle if eventually points back. Is it possible all right-moving blocked? Let's construct M=5, N=3, X=[0,1,5]? gaps1,4. Suppose D1>0,D2>0,D3>0? X3=5, Y3 maybe6? D3>0, X3 left gap4, so D3 blocked left -> D2>0. D2? X2=1, right gap4 (not1), left gap1 (not4? M-1=4), so D2 moving right safe? Actually X2+1=2, left gap2, right gap3, safe. So not blocked. To block D2, need right gap1 or left gap4. If right gap4, not block. If left gap1, not block. So D2 safe. Thus not all blocked. In general, a coordinate with both gaps not extreme can move. If all right-moving blocked, each has at least one adjacent gap extreme. This might imply a safe one at boundary of a run.

Similarly for left-moving. There should always be at least one safe move if any D≠0. This is a known property of "graphical arrangements". Good.

def build(arr, M):
    res=[0]*len(arr)
    res[0]=arr[0]
    for i in range(len(arr)-1):
        res[i+1]=res[i] + (arr[i+1]-arr[i]) % M
    return res

Let's write final code:

import sys

def build(arr, M):
    n = len(arr)
    res = [0]*n
    res[0] = arr[0]
    for i in range(n-1):
        res[i+1] = res[i] + (arr[i+1] - arr[i]) % M
    return res

def main():
    data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
    N, M = data[0], data[1]
    A = data[2:2+N]
    B = data[2+N:2+2*N]

    if M == 2:
        print(0 if A == B else -1)
        return

    P = build(A, M)
    Q = build(B, M)

    xs = [P[i] - Q[i] for i in range(N)]
    xs.sort()
    k0 = xs[N//2] // M

    ans = 10**30
    for k in range(k0-2, k0+3):
        km = k * M
        s = 0
        for pi, qi in zip(P, Q):
            d = pi - qi - km
            if d < 0:
                d = -d
            s += d
        if s < ans:
            ans = s
    print(ans)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

Let D_i = Q_i - P_i. We need find i with D_i>0 and P_i+1 valid, or D_i<0 and P_i-1 valid.

Assume no such i. Define R = {i | D_i>0}, L = {i | D_i<0}. For i in R, moving right invalid. Invalid right means:
(1) P_i+1 = P_{i+1} (right gap 1) OR
(2) P_i - P_{i-1} = M-1 (left gap M-1) (for i>1; for i=1 only right gap? Actually moving P_1 right decreases gap to right, invalid if P_1+1=P_2; left gap none. So for i=1, condition (1). For i=N, condition (2) only? Moving P_N right increases left gap, invalid if P_N-P_{N-1}=M-1.)

Thus every i∈R has a neighbor in R (right if right gap1, left if left gap M-1). This defines a directed graph on R where each node has outdegree at least1. Finite => contains a directed cycle. Suppose edges i->i+1 (right) and i+1->i (left) would require P_{i+1}-P_i=1 and =M-1 simultaneously, impossible for M>2. A longer cycle must alternate directions? A directed cycle in a directed graph whose underlying graph is a path must be a 2-cycle. Thus impossible. Therefore some i∈R has no outgoing edge, contradiction. - If u is integer, k0 = u. Since lower median l ≤ u, k0 ∈ [l,u], so k0 minimizes.

   If l ≤ k0, then k0 ∈ [l,u], minimizes.

Underlying graph path cannot have any cycle except 2-cycle. So contradiction if no 2-cycle. We showed no 2-cycle because a 2-cycle between i and i+1 would require one edge i->i+1 and i+1->i. Edge i->i+1 can only be chosen if g_i=1 (right gap1) for i. Edge i+1->i can only be chosen if for i+1, left gap M-1, i.e., g_i=M-1. Both require g_i=1 and M-1, impossible for M>2. What if i->i+1 chosen due to left gap M-1? No, left gap M-1 gives edge to i-1, not i+1. So yes. Good.

For left moves, 2-cycle between i and i+1 would require i->i+1? Wait for left-moving, invalid left for i can point to i-1 (left gap1) or i+1 (right gap M-1). Edge i->i+1 means right gap M-1 for i. Edge i+1->i means left gap1 for i+1, i.e., right gap1 for i. Both require g_i=M-1 and1, impossible. Good.

Thus proof solid.
